I acknowledge that I would have behaved in a more ideal fashion, if, after I had been forsaken by you, I had turned my face from society, and let the cankerworm of despair slowly destroy whatever life and bloom I had left. But I was young, and society had its charms, so did the prospect of wealth and position, however hollow they may have proved; you who are the master of both this day, because twelve months ago you forsook Evelyn Blake, should be the last to reproach me with them. *I* do not reproach you; I only say let the past be forgotten﻿—”

===Chinese===
“我什么也不想说，”他平静地打断道，俯身拾起她失落的扇子。“在一场既是相逢又是诀别的会面里，我不愿说出任何近于反唇相讥的话。我——”
“慢着，”她突然喊道，伸手来取她的扇子，那姿势既高傲又坚决。“您说出了一个字，这字非解释不可；我究竟对您做过什么，您竟对我用起‘反唇相讥’这个字来？”
“做过什么？您动摇了我对女人的信心；您叫我明白，一个女人，既已对一个男人说过她爱他，竟能把这份爱忘到那步田地，为了头衔和珠宝，去嫁一个她永不能敬重的人。您叫我明白——”
“住口，”她又说，这一回没有姿势，也没有动作，只有那双褪成大理石般苍白的嘴唇在动，“那么您又叫我明白了什么？”
他一震，满脸涨得通红，一时间立在她面前，那严冷的镇定剥落净尽。“请您原谅，”他说，“我收回那个字——反唇相讥。”
这回轮到她抬起头来打量他了。她的眼光不及他的冷淡，却同样从容，她望着他朝她俯下的那颗高傲的头，一行一行地研读他的脸，从严峻的额头到紧闭的嘴唇——忧郁仿佛在那唇上打下了永久的火印——她脸上随之掠过一层变化。“霍尔曼，”她说，忽然涌起一阵柔情，“倘若往昔你我行事都太过世故精明，以致今日回首，彼此都觉得算不得多大乐事，难道这就成了缘由，叫我们偏要在那桩旧事上纠缠不休，毁掉整个将来？那桩事，我们如今这年纪，纵使忘不掉，总还埋得掉。我承认，我原该表现得更有理想些：被你抛弃之后，便闭门谢客，任绝望的蠹虫慢慢蛀尽我残存的一点生机与颜色。可我那时年轻，交际场自有它的迷人，富贵前程也一样，不管后来证明多么空虚；而你，如今这两样俱全的人，正因一年前你抛弃了伊芙琳·布莱克，才最没有资格拿它们来责备我。*我*不曾责备你；我只说一句，让过去的事过去吧——”

===Original===
“Impossible,” exclaimed he, his whole face darkening with an expression I could not fathom. “What was done at that time cannot be undone. For you and me there is no future. Yes,” he said turning towards her as she made a slight fluttering move of dissent, “*no future*; we can bury the past, but we can not resurrect it. I doubt if you would wish to if we could; as we cannot, of course you will not desire even to converse upon the subject again. Evelyn I wanted to see you *once*, but I do not wish to see you again; will you pardon my plain speaking, and release me?”
“I will pardon your plain speaking, but﻿—” Her look said she would not release him.
He seemed to understand it so, and smiled, but very bitterly. In another moment he had bowed and gone, and she had returned to her crowd of adoring sycophants.

===Chinese===
“不可能，”他喊道，整张脸阴沉下来，蒙上一层我参不透的神色。“往事既成，无可挽回。你我之间没有将来。是的，”见她做出一点轻颤的抗议姿势，他朝她转过脸来，“*没有将来*；过去埋得掉，却复不得活。倘若真能复活，我还疑心你未必愿意；既然不能，你自然连再提这个话头都不会想了。伊芙琳，我只想见你*这一面*，却不想再见了；原谅我直言，放我走，可好？”
“你的直言我原谅，只是——”她的眼色说，她不放。
他像是听懂了，笑了笑，却笑得极苦。转眼间，他已躬身而去；她也回到了她那群谄媚的崇拜者中间。

## VI A Bit of Calico / 六 一块印花布
===Original===
It was about this time that I took up my residence in a sort of lodging-house that occupied the opposite corner to that of Mr. Blake. My room, as I took pains to have it, overlooked the avenue, and from its windows I could easily watch the goings and comings of the gentleman whose movements were daily becoming of more and more interest to me. For set it down to caprice﻿—and men are often as capricious as women﻿—or account for it as you will, his restlessness at this period was truly remarkable. Not a day that he did not spend his time in walking the streets, and that not in his usual aimless gentlemanly fashion, but eagerly and with an intent gaze that roamed here and there, like a bird seeking its prey. It would often be as late as five o’clock before he came in, and if, as now frequently happened, he did not have company to dinner, he was even known to start out again after seven o’clock and go over the same ground as in the morning, looking with strained gaze, that vainly endeavored to appear unconcerned, into the faces of the women that he passed. I not unfrequently followed him at these times as much for my own amusement as from any hope I had of coming upon anything that should aid me in the work before me. But when he suddenly changed his route of travel from a promenade in the fashionable thoroughfares of Broadway and Fourteenth Street to a walk through Chatham Square and the dark, narrow streets of the East side, I began to scent whom the prey might be that he was seeking, and putting every other consideration aside, regularly set myself to dog his steps, as only I, with my innumerable disguises, knew how to do. For three separate days I kept at his heels wherever he went, each day growing more and more astonished if not to say hopeful, as I found myself treading the narrowest and most disreputable streets of the city; halting at the shops of pawnbrokers; peering into the backrooms of liquor shops; mixing with the crowds that infest the corner groceries at nightfall, and even slinking with hand on the trigger of the pistol I carried in my pocket, up dark alleys where every door that swung noiselessly to and fro as we passed, shut upon haunts of such villainy as only is known to us of the police, or to those good souls that for the sake of One whose example they follow, lay aside their fears and sensitiveness to carry light into the dim pits of this wretched world. At first I thought Mr. Blake might have some such reason for the peculiar course he took. But his indifference to all crowds where only men were collected, his silence where a word would have been well received, convinced me it was a woman he was seeking and that with an intentness which blinded him to the commonest needs of the hour. I even saw him once in his hurry and abstraction, step across the body of a child who had fallen face downward on the stones, and that with an expression showing he was utterly unconscious of anything but an obstacle in his path. The strangest part of it all was that he seemed to have no fear. To be sure he took pains to leave his watch at home; but with such a figure and carriage as he possessed, the absence of jewelry could never deceive the eye for a moment as to the fact of his being a man of wealth, and those he went among would do anything for money. Perhaps, like me, he carried a pistol. At all events he shunned no spot where either poverty lay hid or deviltry reigned, his proud stern head bending to enter the lowest doors without a tremble of the haughty lips that remained compressed as by an iron force; except when some poor forlorn creature with flaunting headgear, and tremulous hands, attracted by his bearing would hastily brush against him, when he would turn and look, perhaps speak, though what he said I always failed to catch; after which he would hurry on as if possessed by seven devils. The evenings of those three days were notable also. Two of them he spent in the manner I have described; the third he went to the Windsor House﻿—where the Countess De Mirac had taken rooms﻿—going up to the ladies’ entrance and actually ringing the bell, only to start back and walk up and down on the opposite side of the way, with his hands behind his back, and his head bent, evidently deliberating as to whether he should or should not carry out his original intention of entering. The arrival of a carriage with the stately subject of his deliberations, who from her elaborate costume had seemingly been to some kettledrum or private reception, speedily put an end to his doubts. As the door opened to admit her, I saw him cast one look at her heavily draped person, with its snowy opera-cloak drawn tightly over the sweeping folds of her maize colored silk, and shrink back with what sounded like a sigh of anger or distrust, and without waiting for the closing of the door upon her, turn toward home with a step that hesitated no longer.
The fourth day to my infinite chagrin, I was sick and could not go with him. All I could do was to wrap myself in blankets and sit in my window from which I had the satisfaction of viewing him start as I supposed upon his usual course. The rest of the day was employed in a long, dull waiting for his return, only relieved by casual glimpses of Mrs. Daniels’ troubled face as she appeared at one window or another of the old-fashioned mansion before me. She seemed, too, to be unusually restless, opening the windows and looking out with forlorn cranings of her neck as if she too were watching for her master. Indeed I have no doubt from what I afterwards learned, that she was in a state of constant suspense during these days. Her frequent appearance at the station house, where she in vain sought for some news of the girl in whose fate she was so absorbed, confirmed this. Only the day before I gave myself up to my unreserved espionage of Mr. Blake, she had had an interview with Mr. Gryce in which she had let fall her apprehensions that the girl was dead, and asked whether if that were the case, the police would be likely to come into a knowledge of the fact. Upon being assured that if she had not been privately made way with, there was every chance in their favor, she had grown a little calmer, but before going away had so far forgotten herself as to intimate that if some result was not reached before another fortnight had elapsed, she should take the matter into her own hands and﻿—She did not say what she would do, but her looks were of a very menacing character. It was no wonder, then, that her countenance bore marks of the keenest anxiety as she trod the halls of that dim old mansion, with its dusky corners rich with bronzes and the glimmering shine of ancient brocades, breathing suggestions of loss and wrong; or bent her wrinkled forehead to gaze from the windows for the coming of one whose footsteps were ever delayed. She happened to be looking out, when after a longer stroll than usual the master of the house returned. As he made his appearance at the corner, I saw her hurriedly withdraw her head and hide herself behind the curtain, from which position she watched him as with tired steps and somewhat dejected mien, he passed up the steps and entered the house. Not till the door closed upon him, did she venture to issue forth and with a hurried movement shut the blinds and disappear. This anxiety on her part redoubled mine, and thankful enough was I when on the next day I found myself well enough to renew my operations. To ferret out this mystery, if mystery it was﻿—I still found myself forced to admit the possibility of there being none﻿—had now become the one ambition of my life; and all because it was not only an unusually blind one, but of a nature that involved danger to my position as detective, I entered upon it with a zest rare even to me who love my work and all it involves with an undivided passion.
To equip myself, then, in a fresh disguise and to join Mr. Blake shortly after he had left his own corner, was anything but a hardship to me that bright winter morning, though I knew from past experience, a long and wearisome walk was before me with nothing in all probability at the end but reiterated disappointment. But for once the fates had willed it otherwise. Whether Mr. Blake, discouraged at the failure of his own attempts, whatever they were, felt less heart to prosecute them than usual I cannot say, but we had scarcely entered upon the lower end of the Bowery, before he suddenly turned with a look of disgust, and gazing hurriedly about him, hailed a Madison Avenue car that was rapidly approaching. I was at that moment on the other side of the way, but I hurried forward too, and signaled the same car. But just as I was on the point of entering it I perceived Mr. Blake step hastily back and with his eyes upon a girl that was hurrying past him with a basket on her arm, regain the sidewalk with a swiftness that argued his desire to stop her. Of course I let the car pass me, though I did not dare approach him too closely after my late conspicuous attempt to enter it with him. But from my stand on the opposite curbstone I saw him draw aside the girl, who from her garments might have been the daughter or wife of any one of the shiftless, drinking wretches lounging about on the four corners within my view, and after talking earnestly with her for a few moments, saunter at her side down Broome Street, still talking. Reckless at this sight of the consequences which might follow his detection of the part I was playing, I hasted after them, when I was suddenly disconcerted by observing him hurriedly separate from the girl and turn towards me with intention as it were to regain the corner he had left. Weighing in an instant the probable good to be obtained by following either party, I determined to leave Mr. Blake for one day to himself, and turn my attention to the girl he had addressed, especially as she was tall and thin and bore herself with something like grace.

===Chinese===
大约就在这时候，我在一家寄宿公寓里住了下来，那公寓正占着布莱克先生宅子对面的街角。我特意费了些周折，要了一间临街的屋子，从窗口可以毫不费力地监视这位先生的出入——他的动静，一天比一天更牵动我的心。任凭你把这说成心血来潮——男人的反复无常原不输女人——或随你怎么解释，他这一阵的坐立不安实在惹眼。他没有一天不把工夫消在逛街之上，而且不是他素来那种绅士派头的闲逛，而是急切切的，一双眼睛东张西望，像一只觅食的鸟。他往往到五点钟才回府；若是——如今这是常事——晚餐没有客陪，据知他甚至七点过后又要出门，把早晨走过的地界重走一遍，用一种竭力装出漠然却装不像的紧张目光，端详迎面走过的每一个女人的脸。这样的时刻，我常跟在他身后，一半为自家的消遣，一半也存着万一撞见什么有助于手头案子的指望。可等到他忽然改了路线，不再在百老汇和十四街那些时髦大街上闲步，却逛起查塔姆广场和东区那些黑暗狭窄的街巷来，我便嗅出他所觅的猎物大约是谁了；于是把别的一切置之度外，正正经经钉起他的梢来——这一道，唯有我，凭着我那数不清的化装术，最是拿手。接连三天，他到哪里我到哪里；一天比一天吃惊，甚至暗暗生出希望：我发现自己踏进了全城最窄、最不体面的街巷；在当铺门口驻足；向酒馆的后屋张望；混进黄昏时分麇集在街角杂货铺前的人群；甚至手扣着口袋里手枪的扳机，侧身溜进黑黢黢的巷子——我们走过时，那些悄然开合的门扇背后，尽是只有我们当警察的才知道的匪窟，或是只有那些善人才知道的去处——他们为了效法他们主的榜样，撇下恐惧与羞怯，把光亮送进这悲惨世界的一个个暗坑。起初我还想，布莱克先生拣这条古怪路线，或许也怀着这样的用心。可他对一切只有男人聚着的群伙一概漠然，在说一句话便可大受欢迎的地方一言不发，这使我确信，他寻的是一个女人，而且寻得那样专注，连眼下最寻常的需要都视而不见了。我甚至亲眼见过一回：他匆忙出神之间，从一个脸朝下跌倒在石板上的孩子身上跨了过去，那副神情表明，他浑然不觉那是个孩子，只当是路上的一重障碍。最奇怪的是，他仿佛全无惧色。不错，他小心把手表留在家里；可凭他那样的身量和气派，纵然不佩一件首饰，谁也一眼看得出他是位财主，而他混迹的那班人，为了钱什么事都干得出来。也许他也同我一样，揣着一把手枪。总之，凡是穷困潜藏或罪恶横行的地方，他没有一处不肯去；他那颗高傲冷峻的头颅低下来钻进最卑贱的门户，两片傲慢的嘴唇依旧紧闭如铁，纹丝不动。只是偶尔有哪个可怜落寞的女人——头上插着招摇的花哨头饰，双手哆嗦，被他那气派所引——匆匆擦他一下，他这才回头看一看，也许还搭句话，虽然他说什么我始终没能听清；过后他便急急赶路，仿佛身上附着七个鬼。那三个晚上的事也值得一提。两个照我说的样子度过；第三个晚上他去了温莎旅馆——德·米拉克伯爵夫人在那里开了房间——走到女客入口，居然按响了门铃，却蓦地缩回，退到街对面来回踱步，双手背在身后，头低着，显然正自盘算：进，还是不进。正在这时，一辆马车载来了他盘算的那位雍容的主儿——瞧她那身讲究的衣装，多半是从什么茶会或私宅招待会上归来——他的踌躇顿时烟消云散。车门开了让她进去，我看见他朝她那裹得厚厚的身形投去一瞥——雪白的风氅紧紧罩在玉米黄绸裙曳地的褶裥上——缩了一下，像是发出一声愤怒或不信的叹息；不等门在她身后关上，他便转身朝家走去，脚步再无一丝迟疑。
第四天，我竟病了，不能随他去，懊恼得无以复加。我只能裹着毯子坐在窗口，还算满意地望着他照我猜想的往常路线出发。这一天余下的时光，便在枯坐等他归来中捱过，唯一的调剂，是偶尔瞥见丹尼尔斯太太那张愁苦的脸，在眼前那座老式宅子的一扇扇窗前出现。她也显得异样地不安生，开了窗，伸长脖子凄惶地朝外张望，仿佛她也在盼着主人。的确，凭我后来知道的事，我毫不怀疑她这几日无时无刻不在悬心。她频频到警局走动，想打听一点那姑娘的消息而不可得——那姑娘的命运，她是全副心神都系在上头的——这便是一个明证。就在我着手毫无顾忌地盯布莱克先生梢的头一天，她还同格赖斯先生见过一面，吐露出她的忧虑，说姑娘只怕已经死了，又问倘若如此，警察可有望查明实情。得了那句担保——只要姑娘不是被人私下害了，他们便有十足的把握——她才略略安了些心；可临走之前，她竟失态到这般地步：声言倘若再过两个礼拜还无结果，她便要自家动手来办这案子，还有——她没说要做什么，可那神色着实咄咄逼人。无怪乎她在那座幽暗的老宅里走来走去时，脸上带着最深的焦灼——宅子的暗角里陈着青铜器，闪着古锦缎的微光，处处透着失落与冤屈的气息；也无怪乎她要皱着那满是皱纹的额头，贴着窗子守望一个脚步迟迟不归的人。那天宅子的主人散步得比往常更久，回来时，她恰好在朝外望。他刚在街角露面，我便见她慌忙缩回头，藏到窗帘后面，从那里瞧着他迈着疲惫的脚步、神色颓丧地登上台阶进了宅门。直到门在他身后关上，她才敢露面，匆匆放下百叶窗，人便不见了。她这份焦灼使我那份加了倍；到了第二天，我发现自己身子好些，能够重操旧业，真是谢天谢地。这桩谜案——倘若真是谜案的话；我至今仍不得不承认，它也可能根本无谜——如今成了我此生唯一的志向；正因为它不但是一桩格外难测的谜，而且性质上还牵涉我侦探前程的风险，我着手去做时那份兴头，连我这个以全副热肠爱着本行和它的一切甘苦的人，也属难得。
于是，在那个晴朗的冬日早晨，换上一副新行头、趁布莱克先生刚拐出他家街角便缀上去，于我说来毫不费难，尽管凭往日的经验，我知道前头等着的是一趟漫长乏味的长走，走到头十有八九还是一场扫兴。可这一回，命运偏偏另作了安排。布莱克先生是不是因为自己那番——不管是什么——寻访一无所成，无心再照旧进行，我说不准；总之我们刚踏上包厘街的南段，他忽然面带厌烦地转过身，匆匆四顾，招手唤住一辆疾驰而来的麦迪逊大道街车。我那时正在街对面，也赶紧抢上前，招呼了同一辆车。可正当我要上车，却见布莱克先生急急退了回来，眼睛盯着一个臂上挎篮、匆匆打他身边走过的姑娘，三步并作两步回到人行道上——那急切，分明是想截住她。我自然放那街车走了，只是方才那番惹眼的上车姿态之后，我不敢再逼近他。但从我站的对面街缘石上，我望见他把这姑娘拉到一旁——看她那身衣裳，她尽可以是四下可见的那些游手好闲的醉鬼泼皮之中任何一人的女儿或老婆——同她恳切地谈了几句，便在她身侧缓缓而行，一路往布卢姆街去了，嘴里说着话。眼见这一幕，我顾不得被他识破我所扮角色会有什么后患，拔脚便追；不想他又突然同那姑娘匆匆分开，朝我这边转过来，像是要回到他方才离开的街角，害得我一时着慌。转念间把跟踪两造的得失掂了一掂，我决意这一天且由布莱克先生自去，把注意力转到他搭话的那个姑娘身上，何况她又高又瘦，举止间还带几分优雅。

===Original===
Barely bestowing a glance upon him, then, as he passed, in a vain attempt to read the sombre expression of his inscrutable face grown five years older in the last five days, I shuffled after the girl now flitting before me down Broome Street. As I did so, I noticed her dress to its minutest details, somewhat surprised to find how ragged and uncouth it was. That Mr. Blake should stop a girl wherever seen, clad in a black alpaca frock, a striped shawl and a Bowery hat trimmed with feathers, I could easily understand; but that this creature with her faded calico dress, dingy cape thrown carelessly over her head, and ragged basket, should arrest his attention, was a riddle to me. I hastened forward with intent to catch a glimpse of her countenance if possible; but she seemed to have acquired wings to her feet since her interview with Mr. Blake. Darting into a crowd of hooting urchins that were rushing from Centre Street after a broken wagon and runaway horse, she sped from my sight with such rapidity, I soon saw that my only hope of overtaking her lay in running. I accordingly quickened my steps when those same hooting youngsters getting in the way of my feet, I tripped up and﻿—well, I own I retired from that field baffled. Not entirely so, however. Just as I was going down, I caught sight of the girl tearing away from a box of garbage on the curbstone; and when order having been restored, by which lofty statement I mean to say when your humble servant had regained his equilibrium, I awoke to the fact that she had effectually disappeared, I hurried to that box and succeeded in finding hanging to it a bit of rag easily recognized as a piece of the old calico frock of nameless color which I had been following a moment before. Regarding it as the sole spoils of a very unsatisfactory day’s work, I put it carefully away in my pocket book, where it lay till﻿—But with all my zeal for compression, I must not anticipate.
When I came home that afternoon I found myself unexpectedly involved in a matter that for the remainder of the day at least, prevented me from further attending to the affair I had in hand. The next morning Mr. Blake did not start out as usual, and at noon I received intimation from Fanny that he was preparing to take a journey. Where, she could not inform me, nor when, though she thought it probable he would take an early train. Mrs. Daniels was feeling dreadfully, she informed me; and the house was like a grave. Greatly excited at this unexpected move on Mr. Blake’s part, I went home and packed my valise with something of the spirit of her who once said, under somewhat different circumstances I allow, “Whither thou goest I will go.”
The truth was, I had travelled so far and learned so little, that my professional pride was piqued. That expression of Mr. Gryce still rankled, and nothing could soothe my injured spirit now but success. Accordingly when Mr. Blake stepped up to the ticket office of the Hudson River Railroad next morning, to buy a ticket for Putney, a small town in the northern part of Vermont, he found beside him a spruce young drummer, or what certainly appeared such, who by some strange coincidence, wanted a ticket for the same place. The fact did not seem in the least to surprise him, nor did he cast me a look beyond the ordinary glance of one stranger at another. Indeed Mr. Blake had no appearance of being a suspicious man, nor do I think at this time, he had the remotest idea that he was either watched or followed; an ignorance of the truth which I took care to preserve by taking my seat in a different car from him and not showing myself again during the whole ride from New York to Putney.

===Chinese===
他走过时，我只匆匆瞥了他一眼，想读一读他那张莫测高深的脸上那副沉郁神色——这五天里，那张脸老了五年——却是徒然；随即我便拖着脚步，跟上前面那姑娘，她正顺着布卢姆街翩然前行。一面走，我把她的衣着从头到脚打量了个仔细，见其褴褛粗陋，不免有些诧异。布莱克先生见着一个穿黑羽纱裙子、条纹披肩、戴插羽毛的包厘街帽子的姑娘，无论在哪里都要拦下，这我好懂；可眼前这个人，褪色的印花布裙子，头上胡乱罩着一件乌旧的短斗篷，挎着个破篮子，竟也能攫住他的注意，于我却是一桩谜。我加紧赶上，想若有可能觑一觑她的脸；可她自从同布莱克先生打过交道，脚下便像生了翅。她一头扎进一群起哄的孩子堆里——他们正从中央街追着一辆摔坏的货车和一匹惊马跑来——那股快劲，转眼便出了我的视界；我随即明白，要追上她，唯有拔腿飞奔。我于是加快脚步，偏偏那群起哄的小子绊在我脚前，我一个趔趄——唉，我认了，我在这战场上败下阵来。不过也不算全败。就在我栽倒的当口，我瞥见那姑娘正从街缘石上的一只垃圾箱旁飞奔而去；等到秩序恢复——说这句大话，意思不过是敝人重新找回了重心——我醒悟她已经跑得没了影，便赶到那只箱子跟前，居然寻见上头挂着一条破布，一望便知是我方才一路跟定的那件说不上颜色的旧印花布裙子上的一角。我权当它是一天窝囊差使里唯一的战利品，仔仔细细收进皮夹，它便在那里躺着，一直躺到——罢了，我虽一心求简，也不可预先抖搂。
那天下午回到家，我意外地卷进了一桩事里，至少当天余下的时辰，再顾不上手头的案子。第二天早晨，布莱克先生没有照常出门；晌午时分，范妮给我捎来消息，说他正预备出一趟远门。去哪里，她说不上，几时走也说不准，只怕要搭早班火车。丹尼尔斯太太难受得厉害，她告诉我；宅子里死气沉沉，像一座坟。布莱克先生这一手来得突然，我大为兴奋，回家收拾了旅行包，那劲头，颇有几分像当年那位说过“你往哪里去，我也往那里去”的女子的气概——我承认，情形是有些不同的。
实情是：我跑了这么远的路，知道的却这么少，职业的自尊着实被刺痛了。格赖斯先生那句话至今还硌在心里；如今除非成功，再没有什么能抚平我受伤的心气。于是，第二天早晨，当布莱克先生走到哈德逊河铁路的售票窗前，要买一张去佛蒙特州北部小镇普特尼的车票时，他身旁正站着一个衣履光鲜的年轻行商——或者至少看去如此——说来也怪，这人偏偏也要买一张去同一地方的车票。这事似乎丝毫没有叫他惊奇，他看我，也不过陌生人之间寻常的一瞥。说实在的，布莱克先生毫无多疑之人的模样；而且我想，他这时做梦也想不到自己正被人盯着、跟着；这份懵懂，我又刻意替他保全——我坐进另一节车厢，从纽约到普特尼的一路，再没有露过面。

## VII The House at the Granby Cross Roads / 七 格兰比岔口的宅子

===Original===
Why Mr. Blake should take a journey at all at this time, and why of all places in the world he should choose such an insignificant town as Putney for his destination, was of course the mystery upon which I brooded during the entire distance. But when somewhere near five in the afternoon I stepped from the cars on to the platform at Putney Station only to hear Mr. Blake making inquiries in regard to a certain stage running between that town and a still smaller village further east, I own I was not only surprised but well-nigh nonplussed. Especially as he seemed greatly disappointed to hear that it only ran once a day, and then for an earlier train in the morning.

“You will have to wait till tomorrow I fear,” said the ticket agent, “unless the landlord of the hotel down yonder, can harness you up a team. There is a funeral out west today and﻿—”

I did not wait to hear more but hurried down to the hotel he had pointed out, and hunting up the landlord inquired if for love or money he could get me any sort of a conveyance for Melville that afternoon. He assured me it would be impossible, the livery stable as well as his own being entirely empty.

“Such a thing don’t happen here once in five years,” said he to me. “But the old codger who is dead, though a queer dick was a noted personage in these parts, and not a man, woman or child, who could find a horse, mule or donkey, but what availed himself of the privilege. Even the doctor’s spavined mare was pressed into service, though she halts on one leg and stops to get her breath half a dozen times in going up one short hill. You will have to wait for the stage, sir.”

===Chinese===
布莱克先生何以偏在这个时候出远门，普天之下地方这许多，又何以偏偏拣中帕特尼这样一个不起眼的小镇作目的地，自然是我一路盘算不休的谜。可是等到下午五点来钟，我在帕特尼站下车走上站台，却听见布莱克先生正在打听一班驿车——往来本镇与东边一个更小村子之间的驿车——我得承认，我不但吃了一惊，简直几乎被弄得没了主意。尤其见他听说这车一天只开一趟，还是专接早晨更早一班的火车，脸上分明大失所望。

“恐怕您只好等到明天了，”票房里那位说，“除非坡下那家客栈的老板能给您套起一挂车。今天西边有人家出殡，所以——”

我没等他往下说，便赶下坡去，到他说的那家客栈，寻着老板，问他不论讲交情还是使钱，能否当天下午替我张罗到随便什么去梅尔维尔的代步。他一口担保万万不能：车马行连同他自家的牲口，早已一空。

“这样的事，咱们这儿五年也轮不上一回，”他对我说。“不过死去的那位老小子，人虽古怪，在这一带倒算得上个人物；凡寻得着马、骡子、驴子的，不分男女老小，没有一个不趁这个机会风光一趟的。连医生那匹腿上有毛病的母马也被拉了差，虽说它瘸着一条腿，上一道小坡也要停下六回喘气。您只有等驿车了，先生。”

===Original===
“But I am in a hurry,” said I as I saw Mr. Blake enter. “I have business in Melville tonight, and I would pay anything in reason to get there.”

But the landlord only shook his head; and drawing back with the air of an abused man, I took up my stand in the doorway where I could hear the same colloquy entered into with Mr. Blake, with the same unsatisfactory termination. He did not take it quite as calmly as I did, though he was of too reserved a nature to display much emotion over anything. The prospect of a long tedious evening spent in a country hotel seemed almost unendurable to him, but he finally succumbed to the force of circumstances, as indeed he seemed obliged to do, and partaking of such refreshment as the rather poorly managed hotel afforded, retired without ceremony to his room, from which he did not emerge again till next morning. In all this he had somehow managed not to give his name; and by means of some inquiries I succeeded in making that evening, I found his person was unknown in the town.

By a little management I secured the next room to his, by which arrangement I succeeded in passing a sleepless night, Mr. Blake spending most of the wee sma’ hours in pacing the floor of his room, with an unremitting regularity that had anything but a soothing effect upon my nerves. Early the next morning we took the stage, he sitting on the back seat, and I in front with the driver. There were other passengers, but I noticed he never spoke to any of them, nor through all the long drive did he once look up from the corner where he had ensconced himself. It was twelve o’clock when we reached the end of the route, a small town of somewhat less than the usual pretensions of mountain villages; so insignificant indeed, that I found it more and more difficult to imagine what the wealthy ex-Congressman could find in such a spot as this, to make amends for a journey of such length and discomfort; when to my increasing wonder I heard him give orders for a horse to be saddled and brought round to the inn door directly after dinner. This was a move I had not expected and it threw me a little aback, for although I had thus far managed to hold myself so aloof from Mr. Blake, even while keeping him under my eye, that no suspicion of my interest in his movements had as yet been awakened, how could I thus for the third time follow his order with one precisely similar, without attracting an attention that would be fatal to my plans. Yet to let him ride off alone now, would be to drop the trail at the very moment the scent became of importance.

===Chinese===
“可我赶时间啊，”我说这话时，正看见布莱克先生走进来。“我今夜在梅尔维尔有正事，只要价钱在理，叫我出多少都成。”

但老板只是摇头；我作出一副受了怠慢的神气退后几步，在门口站定，正好把同一番问答在布莱克先生身上又听了一遍，得到同样不如人意的结局。他不像我这样处之泰然，只是天性矜持，不论什么事都不大显露于外。要在一间乡下客栈里消磨一个漫长乏味的夜晚，这前景在他几乎难以忍受；但他终究拗不过形势——他也实在别无办法——将就吃了些这家管理不善的客栈所能供应的饭食，便一言不发回房去了，直到次日早晨才再露面。这一番经过，他居然始终不曾泄露姓名；当晚我设法做了几处打探，才知道全镇无人识得他这个人。

略施小计，我要到了他隔壁的房间；这一安排的结果，是我彻夜未曾合眼——布莱克先生把后半夜的大半工夫，都花在他房里踱步上，那步子匀净得一丝不苟，对我这边的神经却丝毫没有抚慰之效。第二天清早我们上了驿车，他坐后座，我在前面同车夫并排。车上另有几名乘客，但我留意到他同谁也不搭话，一路长途颠簸，也从不曾从他安身的那个角落里抬一次眼。到得路线尽头已是十二点，那是一座山镇，气派比寻常山村还要小些；这地方委实不起眼，我越想越不明白，这位家资巨万的前国会议员在此地能寻着什么，足以补偿这一路的长途劳顿；不料更叫我诧异的是，我听见他吩咐备马，一吃过饭就牵到客栈门口来。这一着我全没料到，不由得有些着慌：虽然到此为止，我始终同布莱克先生保持距离——眼睛却盯住他——还不曾惹起他半点疑心，叫我留意起他的行动，可我若第三次随着他的吩咐下一个一模一样的吩咐，怎能不招人注目、葬送我的全盘计划？然而此刻若任他单人独骑而去，便是在踪迹正要紧的关口，把线索丢了。

===Original===
The landlord, a bustling, wiry little man all nervousness and questions, unwittingly helped me at this crisis.

“Are you going on to Perry, sir?” inquired he of that gentleman, “I have been expecting a man along these three days bound for Perry.”

“I am that man,” I broke in, stepping forward with some appearance of asperity, “and I hope you won’t keep me waiting. A horse as soon as dinner is over, do you hear? I am two days late now, and won’t stand any nonsense.”

===Chinese===
老板是个风风火火的干瘦小个子，一身的急惊风和好奇心，在这紧要关头无意之中帮了我一把。

“您还要往前去佩里吗，先生？”他向那位绅士问道。“我这三天一直巴望着有个去佩里的人来呢。”

“去佩里的正是在下，”我插口道，跨上一步，装出几分气恼，“望您别叫我久等。饭一吃完就要马，听见没有？我已经迟了两天，可没心思听人打哈哈。”

===Original===
And to escape the questions sure to follow, I strode into the dining-room with a half-fierce, half-sullen countenance, that effectually precluded all advances. During the meal I saw Mr. Blake’s eye roam more than once towards my face; but I did not return his gaze, or notice him in any way; hurrying through my dinner, and mounting the first horse brought around, as if time were my only consideration. But once on the road I took the first opportunity to draw rein and wait, suddenly remembering that I had not heard Mr. Blake give any intimation of the direction he intended taking. A few minutes revealed to me his elegant form well mounted and showing to perfection in his closely buttoned coat, slowly approaching up the road. Taking advantage of a rise in the ground, I lingered till he was almost upon me, when I cantered quickly on, fearing to arouse his apprehensions if I allowed him to pass me on a road so solitary as that which now stretched out before us: a move provocative of much embarassment to me, as I dared not turn my head for the same reason, anxious as I was to keep him in sight.

The roads dividing before me, at length gave me my first opportunity to pause and look back. He was some fifty paces behind. Waiting till he came up, I bowed with the surly courtesy I thought in keeping with the character I had assumed, and asked if he knew which road led towards Perry, saying I had come off in such haste I had forgotten to inquire my way. He returned my bow, pointed towards the left hand road and saying, “I know this does *not*,” calmly took it.

Now here was a dilemma. If in face of this curt response I proceeded to follow him, my hand was revealed at once; yet the circumstances would admit of no other course. I determined to compromise matters by pretending to take the right hand road till he was out of sight, when I would return and follow him swiftly upon the left. Accordingly I reined my horse to the right, and for some fifteen minutes galloped slowly away towards the north; but another fifteen saw me facing the west, and riding with a force and fury of which I had not thought the old mare they had given me capable, till I put her to the test. It was not long before I saw my fine gentleman trotting in front of me up a long but gentle slope that rose in the distance; and slackening my own rein, I withdrew into the forest at the side of the road, till he had passed its summit and disappeared, when I again galloped forward.

===Chinese===
为躲开势必跟来的一连串盘问，我板着一副半凶横半阴沉的面孔大步走进饭厅，这副神气足以杜绝一切攀谈。吃饭中间，我不止一次觉出布莱克先生的目光溜向我这张脸；我却不回望，也绝不理会，只匆匆扒完饭，头一匹马牵过来便跨了上去，仿佛光阴是我唯一的计较。可一上了路，我便趁头一个机会勒缰停住——忽然想起，还不曾听见布莱克先生透露半句他要往哪个方向去。不消几分钟，他那俊雅的身姿便映入我的眼帘：骑坐端正，扣得严严实实的大衣把身段衬得恰到好处，正顺着大路缓缓而来。我借一道隆起的地势磨蹭着，直待他几乎赶到跟前，才催马疾行——我怕的是，倘若任他越我而过，行在前方这般荒僻的路上，反倒要惹他起疑。这一着也令我大为局促，因为同样的缘故，我竟不敢回头张望，心里却又急于不放他出离视线。

面前的道路终于分了岔，这才给了我头一个停步回望的机会。他落后约莫五十来步。我等他赶上来，怀着自以为配得上我这副假身份的粗率客气还了一礼，请问哪一条路通往佩里，只说动身匆忙，忘了问路。他回了我一礼，朝左手那条路一指，说了句“我知道这条**不是**”，便不动声色地打马上了那条路。

这下可为难了。他既这样一句顶了回来，我再跟上去，便是当场亮出底牌；可眼下的形势又容不得另作道理。我决意两全其美：先装作取道右手那条路，待他望不见了，再折回来沿左手一路疾追。于是我拨马向右，往北慢慢溜了约一刻钟；再一刻钟，我已面朝正西，纵马飞驰，那股狠劲猛劲，是我先前不曾指望他们给我的那匹老母马使得出来的——直到逼它一试方知。不多时，我便望见我这位漂亮绅士在前头小跑着，登上一道远处升起的缓长山坡；我松了缰，闪进路旁林中，待他翻过坡顶不见踪影，才再度纵马赶路。

===Original===
And thus we went on for an hour, over the most uneven country I ever traversed, he always one hill ahead; when suddenly, by what instinct I cannot determine, I felt myself approaching the end, and hastening to the top of the ascent up which I was then laboring, looked down into the shallow valley spread out before me.

What a sight met my eyes if I had been intent on anything less practical than the movements of the solitary horseman below! Hills on hills piled about a verdant basin in whose depths nestled a scanty collection of houses, in number so small they could be told upon the fingers of the right hand, but which notwithstanding lent an indescribable aspect of comfort to this remote region of hill and forest.

But the vision of Mr. Blake pausing halfway down the slope before me, examining, yes examining a pistol which he held in his hand, soon put an end to all ideas of romance. Somewhat alarmed I reined back; but his action had evidently no connection with me, for he did not once glance behind him, but kept his eye on the road which I now observed took a short turn towards a house of so weird and ominous an appearance that I scarcely marvelled at his precaution.

===Chinese===
我们就这样走了一个来钟头，走的是我平生所经最坎坷的一片地方，他永远隔着一道山梁在前；忽然间——凭的什么直觉我说不清——我觉出此行将近尽头，便一口气赶上我正苦苦攀行的那面坡顶，朝脚下铺展开的浅谷望去。

倘若我心心念念的不是坡下那个独行者的一举一动，而是任何不那么实惠的东西，这一眼望见的景象该何等叫人叹赏！层峦叠嶂环抱着一泓青翠的盆地，盆地深处偎着寥寥几户人家，数目少得右手屈指便数得过来，却偏给这片山荒林远的去处平添了一种说不出的安适气象。

可惜布莱克先生的身影正在前面半坡上停住，端详着——是的，端详着手心里的一支手枪——这一幅情景，登时把满怀的诗情画意驱得干干净净。我微觉心惊，勒马稍退；但他的举动显然与我无涉，他一次也不曾回头张望，只顾盯住大路——我这才发现，那路短短一拐，通向一座模样好生古怪凶祟的房子，无怪他要这般留神提防。

===Original===
Situated on a level track of land at the crossing of three roads, its spacious front, rude and unpainted as it was, presented every appearance of an inn, but from its moss-grown chimneys no smoke arose, nor could I detect any sign of life in its shutterless windows and closed doors, across which shivered the dark shadow of the one gaunt and aged pine, that stood like a guard beside its tumbled-down porch.

Mr. Blake seemed to have been struck by the same fact concerning its loneliness, for hurriedly replacing his pistol in his breast pocket, he rode slowly forward. I instantly conceived the plan of striking across the belt of underbrush that separated me from this old dwelling, and by taking my stand opposite its front, intercept a view of Mr. Blake as he approached. Hastily dismounting, therefore, I led my horse into the bushes and tied her to a tree, proceeding to carry out my plan on foot. I was so far successful as to arrive at the further edge of the wood, which was thick enough to conceal my presence without being too dense to obstruct my vision, just as Mr. Blake passed on his way to this solitary dwelling. He was looking very anxious, but determined. Turning my eyes from him, I took another glance at the house, which by this movement I had brought directly before me. It was even more deserted-looking than I had thought; its unpainted front with its double row of blank windows meeting your gaze without a response, while the huge old pine with half its limbs dismantled of foliage, rattled its old bones against its sides and moaned in its aged fashion like the solitary retainer of a dead race.

I own I felt the cold shivers creep down my back as that creaking sound struck my ears, though as the day was chill with an east wind I dare say it was more the effect of my sudden cessation from exercise, than of any superstitious awe I felt. Mr. Blake seemed to labor under no such impressions. Riding up to the front door he knocked without dismounting, on its dismal panels with his riding whip. No response was heard. Knitting his brows impatiently, he tried the latch: the door was locked. Hastily running his eye over the face of the building, he drew rein and proceeded to ride around the house, which he could easily do owing to the absence of every obstruction in the way of fence or shrubbery. Finding no means of entrance he returned again to the front door which he shook with an impatient hand that however produced no impression upon the trusty lock, and recognizing, doubtless, the futility of his endeavors, he drew back, and merely pausing to give one other look at its deserted front, turned his horse’s head, and to my great amazement, proceeded with sombre mien and clouded brow to retake the road to Melville.

===Chinese===
那房子坐落在一方平地上，正当三条道路交叉的岔口；宽敞的正面虽则粗陋、未经油漆，却处处是一副客栈的做派；只是苔痕斑斑的烟囱里不冒一缕烟，没窗板的窗子与紧闭的门户间也窥不出半点人气息，唯有一株瘦瘪嶙峋的老松立在坍塌的门廊旁边，像个看守似的，黑沉沉的影子掠过门窗，簌簌打颤。

这宅子孤寂无人的情形，布莱克先生似乎也同样看在眼里，只见他慌忙把那支手枪揣回胸袋，缓缓催马上前。我立刻心生一计：斜穿隔在我与这座老屋之间的那带灌木丛，到它正面站定，迎头截看布莱克先生过来的动静。于是我急忙下马，把马牵进树丛拴在一棵树上，徒步去施行我的计划。总算顺利，待我赶到林子那一头——那林子密得足以藏住我的身形，又不致密得遮断我的视线——布莱克先生恰正走向这座孤宅。他满面焦灼，却又是主意已定的神气。我把目光从他身上移开，再朝那房子望去——经这一挪动，它已端端正正立在我眼前了。它比我先前所想的还要荒凉；未上漆的正面，两排空窗迎着你的目光，全无回应；那株高大的老松，半边枝叶凋残，一身老骨头磕在屋墙上格格作响，又以它老迈的腔调呜咽自语，活像绝了嗣的一族剩下的最后一名老仆。

我承认，那吱吱嘎嘎的声音送进耳朵时，一股寒栗顺着脊背爬了下来；不过那日东风料峭，我想这多半是骤然歇了手脚的缘故，并非我心里真存着什么迷信的敬畏。布莱克先生似乎全无这等感触。他策马直抵大门，并不下鞍，扬起马鞭在那阴凄凄的门板上叩了几下。寂然无应。他不耐烦地锁起眉头去试门闩：门是锁着的。他把这楼房的正面匆匆一扫，带转缰绳，便绕着屋子骑行起来——四围没有围栏灌木一类的障碍，绕行毫不费事。见寻不出进路，他又回到正门，焦躁地伸手摇撼；那把牢靠的锁却不为所动。想必他也认了自己这番徒劳，便退开一步，只再对那荒凉的门面望了一眼，拨转马头，面色阴沉、眉头深锁，竟自取来路回梅尔维尔去了，大出我的意外。
===Original===
This old inn or decayed homestead was then the object of his lengthened and tedious journey; this ancient house rotting away among the bleak hills of Vermont, the bourne towards which his steps had been tending for these past two days. I could not understand it. Rapidly emerging from the spot where I had secreted myself, I in my turn made a circuit of the house, if happily I should discover some loophole of entrance which had escaped his attention. But every door and window was securely barred, and I was about to follow his example and leave the spot, when I saw two or three children advancing towards me down the cross roads, gaily swinging their school books. I noticed they hesitated and huddled together as they approached and saw me, but not heeding this, I accosted them with a pleasant word or so, then pointing over my shoulder to the house behind, asked who lived there. Instantly their already pale faces grew paler.

“Why,” cried one, a boy, “don’t you know? That is where the two wicked men lived who stole the money out of the Rutland bank. They were put in prison, but they got away and﻿—”

Here, the other, a little girl, plucked him by the sleeve with such affright, that he himself took alarm and just giving me one quick stare out of his wide eyes, grasped his companion by the hand and took to his heels. As for myself I stood rooted to the ground in my astonishment. This blank, sleepy old house the home of the notorious Schoenmakers after whom half of the detectives of the country were searching? I could scarcely credit my own ears. True I now remembered they had come from these parts, still﻿—

===Chinese===
原来这座老旧的客栈、这片破败的家宅，便是他这一场漫长劳顿旅行的目标；这座在佛蒙特荒山间朽坏着的老屋，便是他这两天一路奔赴的归宿。我委实不解。我急忙从藏身之处钻出，也绕着屋子走了一遭，指望侥幸寻出一个他看漏了的进口。可是门窗都封得严严实实，我正要学他的样离开此地，忽见两三个孩子顺着岔路朝我走来，欢快地甩着书本。我留意到他们走近望见我时，迟疑着挤作了一团；我不理会这个，和颜悦色地搭讪了一两句，便指指肩后那座房子，问里头住的是谁。霎时间，他们本就苍白的脸越发白了。

“咦，”一个男孩儿嚷道，“您不知道吗？偷了拉特兰银行钱的那两个坏人，就住在那儿。他们关进了大牢，可是又跑掉了，后来——”

话音未落，另一个孩子，一个小姑娘，惊惶地一把揪住他的袖子，他自己也着了慌，睁大的眼睛飞快地瞪我一眼，拽起同伴的手撒腿便跑。至于我，一时惊得钉在了地上。这座空落落、昏沉沉的老房子，竟是那恶名昭著的舍恩马克父子的老巢——全国半数侦探都在缉拿他们？我简直不敢相信自己的耳朵。不错，我这时想起来了，他们原是这一带的人氏，可是……

===Original===
Turning round I eyed the house once more. How altered it looked to me! What a murderous aspect it wore, and how dismally secret were the tight shut windows and closely fastened doors, on one of which a rude cross scrawled in red chalk met the eye with a mysterious significance. Even the old pine had acquired the villainous air of the uncanny repositor of secrets too dreadful to reveal, as it groaned and murmured to itself in the keen east wind. Dark deeds and foul wrong seemed written all over the fearful place, from the long strings of black moss that clung to the worm-eaten eaves, to the worn stone with its great blotch of something﻿—could it have been blood?﻿—that served as a threshold to the door. Suddenly with the quickness of lightning the thought flashed across me, what could Mr. Blake, the aristocratic representative of New York’s oldest family, have wanted in this nest of infamy? What errand of hope, fear, despair, avarice or revenge, could have brought this superior gentleman with his refined tastes and proudly reticent manners, so many miles from home, to the forsaken den of a brace of hardy villains whose name for two years now, had stood as the type of all that was bold, bad and lawless, and for whom during the last six weeks the prison had yawned, and the gallows hungered. Contemplation brought no reply, and shocked at my own thoughts, I put the question by for steadier brains than mine; and instead of trying further to solve it, cast about how I was to gain entrance into this deserted building; for to enter it I was more than ever determined, now that I had heard to whom it had once belonged.

Examining with a glance the several roads that branched off in every direction from where I stood, I found them all equally deserted. Even the school children had disappeared in some one of the four or five houses scattered in the remote distance.

If I was willing to enter upon any daring exploit, there was no one to observe or interrupt. I resolved to make the attempt with which my mind was full. This was to climb the old tree, and from one of the two or three branches that brushed against the house, gain entrance at an open garret window that stared at me from amid the pine’s dark needles. Taking off my coat with a sigh over the immaculate condition of my new cassimere trousers, I bent my energies to the task. A difficult one you will say for a city lad, but thanks to fortune I was not brought up in New York, and know how to climb trees with the best. With little more than a scratch or so, I reached the window of which I have spoken, and after a moment spent in regaining my breath, gave one spring and accomplished my purpose. I alighted upon a heap of broken glass in a large bare room. An ominous chill at once struck to my heart. Though I am anything but a sensitive man as far as physical impressions are concerned, there was something in the hollow echo that arose from the four blank walls about me as my feet alighted on that rough, uncarpeted floor, that struck a vague chill through my blood, and I actually hesitated for the moment whether to pursue the investigations I had promised myself, or beat a hasty retreat. A glance at the huge distorted limbs swaying across the square of the open window decided me. It was easy to enter by means of that unsteady support, but it would be extremely unsafe to venture forth in that way. If I prized life and limb I must seek some other method of egress. I at once put my apprehensions in my pocket and entered upon my self imposed task.

A single glance was sufficient to exhaust the resources of the empty garret in which I found myself. Two or three old chairs piled in one corner, a rusty stove or so, a heap of tattered and decaying clothing, were all that met my gaze. Taking my way, then, at once to the ladder, whose narrow ends projecting above a hole in the garret floor, seemed to proffer the means of reaching the rooms below, I proceeded to descend into what to my excited imagination looked like a gulf of darkness. It proved, however, to be nothing more nor less than an unlighted hall of small dimensions, with a staircase at one end and a door at the other, which, upon opening I found myself in a large, square room whose immense four-post bedstead entirely denuded of its usual accompaniments of bed and bolster at once struck my eye and for a moment held it enchained. There were other articles in the room; a disused bureau, a rocking chair, even a table, but nothing had such a ghostly look as that antique bedstead with its curtains of calico tied back over its naked framework, like rags draped from the bare bones of a skeleton. Passing hurriedly by, I tried a closet door or so, finding little, however, to reward my search; and eager to be done with what was every moment becoming more and more drearisome, I hastened across the floor to the front of the house where I found another hall and a row of rooms that, while not entirely stripped of furniture, were yet sufficiently barren to offer little encouragement to my curiosity. One only, a small but not uncomfortable apartment, showed any signs of having been occupied within a reasonable length of time; and as I paused before its hastily spread bed, thrown together as only a man would do it, and wondering why the room was so dark, looked up and saw that the window was entirely covered by an old shawl and a couple of heavy coats that had been hastily nailed across it, I own I felt my hand go to my breast pocket almost as if I expected to see the wild faces of the dreaded Schoenmakers start up all aglare from one of the dim corners before me. Rushing to the window, I tore down with one sweep of my arm both coat and shawl, and with a start discovered that the window still possessed its draperies in the shape of a pair of discolored and tattered curtains tied with ribbons that must once have been brilliant and cheery of color.

===Chinese===
我回过身来，再把这座房子打量一番。它在我眼里显得多么两样了！它带着一股何等凶煞的气象，那紧闭的窗、紧闩的门又藏着何等阴郁的秘密；其中一扇门上，一个用红粉笔涂抹的粗糙十字赫然在目，透着一种神秘的意味。连那株老松，在凛冽的东风里呻吟自语，也添了一身奸邪之气，像个收藏着骇人听闻、不敢宣之于口的隐秘的家伙。这可怖的地方，从扒在虫蛀屋檐上的长长的黑苔，到权充门槛的那块磨旧石头——上头一大摊斑渍，莫非是血？——似乎处处写满了黑暗的勾当与污浊的罪愆。忽然，一个念头闪电般掠过我的脑际：布莱克先生，纽约最古老门第的贵族代表，到这罪恶渊薮里来究竟所求何事？是怎样一桩怀着希望、恐惧、绝望、贪欲或复仇之念的差使，能把这位趣味高雅、举止傲慢缄默的上等绅士，从家中引来这许多英里之外，来到两个亡命恶棍弃置的巢穴？这两人的名字，两年来一直是大胆、凶恶、无法无天的标本；过去六个星期里，牢狱为他们张着大口，绞架为他们饿着肚肠。冥想没有给我回答；我被自己的念头骇住了，便把这问题搁过一旁，留给比我更沉着的头脑；不再试图解开它，转而盘算起如何进这座荒宅——因为如今既已听说它的旧主是何人，我愈发打定主意，非进去看个究竟不可。

我立在原地，扫一眼从我站立处分向四方岔开的几条道路，条条一样荒寂无人。连那几个学童，也已在远处疏疏落落的四五座房子中的某一座里没了影儿。

只要我肯干一桩泼胆的勾当，眼前便无人窥伺，也无人搅扰。我决意放手一试——这念头早塞满了我的心。那就是爬上那株老树，借着擦着屋身的两三根枝桠中的一根，翻进一扇敞开的顶楼窗——那窗正从松树深黑的针叶间瞪着我。我一面叹惜我那条崭新笔挺的呢裤，一面脱下外衣，铆足了劲干将起来。您要说，这对城里哥儿们是桩难事；所幸天可怜见，我不是在纽约长大的，爬树的本事不输给任何人。不过蹭破一两处皮，我便够着了方才说的那扇窗；喘匀了一口气，纵身一跃，竟如其愿。我落在空旷大屋里的一堆碎玻璃上。一阵不祥的寒意霎时透进心底。论身体上的感受，我原算不得一个敏感的人，可是双脚落上那粗糙无毯的地板时，四面空壁间升起的那一片空洞回响，竟教一股莫名的寒气渗进血液，我一时真个踌躇起来：是继续我许下自己的查探，还是速速退出去？望一眼横在敞开的窗洞前摇曳的巨大歪斜的枝干，我拿定了主意。凭那不稳当的支撑进来容易，再那样出去却万分不妥。我若还爱惜这条性命这副手脚，就须另寻出路。我当即把种种疑惧揣进衣袋，着手干起这份自找的差事。

只消一眼，便看尽了我置身其中这间空顶楼的全部家当：墙角摞着两三把旧椅子、一两只锈炉子，再加一堆破烂朽坏的衣裳，便再无一物入眼。我径直走向那架梯子——梯子窄窄的上端从顶楼地板的一个窟窿里探出来，似乎现成指给人到楼下去的路——便往下走去，走进一处在我亢奋的想象中形同黑暗深渊的地方。及至下去，才知不过是一间没有点灯的小小穿堂，一头是楼梯，一头是一扇门；推开门，我已站在一间宽大的方形屋子里，那张高大的四柱床架，褥垫枕褥早剥除净尽，一眼撞来，顷刻间攫住了我的目光。屋里另有几件东西：一张弃置不用的五斗橱，一把摇椅，甚至一张桌子；可是没有什么比那张老式床架更带鬼气——印花布的帐子挽在赤裸裸的床架上，恰似骷髅秃骨上披挂的破布。我匆匆走过，又试开一两扇壁柜门，却寻不出什么值得这一趟辛苦；这地方一刻比一刻阴凄，我巴不得早些了账，便疾步穿过地板，走向宅子前部，那里又有一条穿堂、一排屋子，家具虽未搬尽，却也空荒得叫我的好奇心提不起劲。只有一间，窄小倒也不难受用，看得出不久之前还有人住过；我在那张草草铺就的床前站住——被褥胡乱拢作一团，一望而知出自男人的手笔——正自纳闷屋里为何这般昏暗，一抬头，才见整扇窗被一条旧披肩和两件厚外套钉得严严实实。我承认，我的手几乎不由自主地摸向了胸前口袋，仿佛当真料想着，那叫人闻名丧胆的舍恩马克父子狰狞的面孔，会从我面前哪一个昏暗的角落里霍然亮起。我抢到窗前，一挥胳膊，将外套披巾一并扯下，这才吃了一惊地发现：窗上原来还留着帘幔——一副褪了色、扯烂了的窗帘，系窗帘的缎带，想必当年也曾经鲜亮喜人。

===Original===
Nor was this the only sign in the room of a bygone presence that had possessed a taste for something beyond the mere necessities of life. On the grim coarsely papered wall hung more than one picture; cut from pictorial newspapers to be sure, but each and every one, if I may be called a judge of such matters, possessing some quality of expression to commend it to a certain order of taste. They were all strong pictures. Vivid faces of men and women in daring positions; a hunter holding back a jaguar from his throat; a soldier protecting his comrade from the stroke; and most striking of all, a woman lissome as she was powerful, starting aghast and horror stricken from﻿—what? I could not tell; a rough hand had stripped the remainder of the picture from the wall.

A bit of candle and a half sheet of a newspaper lay on the floor. I picked up the paper. It was a *Rutland Herald* and bore the date of *two days before*. As I read I realized what I had done. If these daring robbers were not at this very moment in the house, they had been there, and that within two or three days. The broken panes of glass in the garret above were now explained. I was not the first one who had climbed that creaking pine tree this fall.

Something like a sensible dread of a very possible danger now seized hold of me. If I had stumbled upon these strangely subtle, yet devilishly bold creatures in their secret lair, the pistol I carried was not going to save me. Shut in like a fox in a hole, I had little to hope for, if they once made their appearance at the stairhead or came upon me from any of the dim halls of the crazy old dwelling, which I now began to find altogether too large for my comfort. Stealing cautiously forth from the room in which I had found so much to disconcert me, I crept towards the front staircase and listened. All was deathly quiet. The old pine tree moaned and twisted without, and from time to time the wind came sweeping down the chimney with an unearthly shrieking sound that was weirdly in keeping with the place. But within and below all was still as the tomb, and though in no ways reassured, I determined to descend and have the suspense over at once. I did so, pistol in hand and ears stretched to their utmost to catch the slightest rustle, but no sound came to disturb me, nor did I meet on this lower floor the sign of any other presence in the house but my own. Passing hastily through what appeared to be a sort of rude parlor, I stepped into the kitchen and tried one of the windows. Finding I could easily lift it from the inside, I drew my breath with ease for the first time since I had alighted among the broken glass above, and turning back, deliberately opened the door of the kitchen stove, and looked in. As I half expected, I found a pile of partly charred rags, showing where the wretches had burned their prison clothing, and proceeding further, picked up from the ashes a ring which whether or not they were conscious of having attempted to destroy in this way I cannot say, but which I thankfully put in my pocket against the day it might be required as proof.

Discerning nothing more in that quarter inviting interest, I asked myself if I had nerve to descend into the cellar. Finally concluding that that was more than could be expected from any man in my position, I gave one look of farewell to the damp and desolate walls about me, then with a breath of relief jumped from the kitchen window again into the light and air of day. As I did so I could swear I heard a door within that old house swing on its hinges and softly close. With a thrill I recognized the fact that it came from the cellar.

---

===Chinese===
屋里那位旧日居停，趣味并不止于柴米度日的必需，这也不是仅见的痕迹。在那阴沉沉的粗花墙纸上，挂着不止一幅画；固然都是从画报上剪下来的，可是倘蒙许我在这类事上充个鉴赏家，幅幅都有一点表情上的可取，投合某一种人的口味。全是些笔力泼辣的画：男男女女鲜活的面孔，都处在性命交关的地位上——一个猎户死死撑住扑向他咽喉的美洲豹；一个兵士以身遮护同伴、格开来势；而最触目的一幅，是一个女人，柔韧之中见矫健，满脸惊骇恐怖，从……从什么上面骇然跃起？我说不上来；一只粗暴的手，早把那幅画的其余部分从墙上撕去了。

地板上搁着一小截蜡烛、半张报纸。我拾起报纸：是《拉特兰先驱报》，日期是*两天前*的。一读之下，我才明白自己闯下了什么。这两个胆大包天的强盗，若不是此刻正在屋中，便是来过，而且不出两三天之内。顶楼上那些打碎的玻璃，至此有了分晓：今年秋天，爬那株吱嘎作响的老松的，我不是头一个。

这时，一种对极可能发生之危险的清醒恐惧攫住了我。我若是撞破了这两个诡谲精乖、又凶横大胆的家伙的秘密巢穴，我随身那支手枪是救不了我的。像狐狸堵在洞里一般没了退路，他们只要在楼梯口一露面，或是从这幢破破烂烂的老宅随便哪条昏暗穿堂里向我扑来，我便没有多少指望；这宅子如今在我看来也委实太大，大得叫我心里发毛。我蹑手蹑脚出了那间屡屡叫我心慌的屋子，向前头的楼梯蹭去，侧耳听时，四下死一样的静。老松在外头呻吟扭动，风时时顺着烟囱灌下来，发出一声声非人世的尖啸，同这地方阴森森地正相称。可是屋内楼下，处处静得像坟墓；我虽依旧悬着心，却决意下楼，索性把这口悬气一口气吐尽。我下去了，一手握着手枪，两耳竖到极处，想捉住最轻的一星窸窣；却没有任何声响来搅扰我，楼下这一层，也不见这宅中除我之外另有生人的丝毫痕迹。匆匆穿过一间像是粗陋客堂的屋子，我跨进厨房，试了试一扇窗。见它从里面一掀便开，我自跌进楼上碎玻璃堆以来，头一回舒舒服服地换了口气；回过身，我不慌不忙揭开厨房炉子的炉门，朝里张望。不出所料，里头有一堆烧得半焦的破布，正是这两个恶徒焚烧囚衣之处；再往灰里拨，又拾起一枚戒指——他们可曾晓得自己连它也试图一并烧毁，我说不准——我却感激似的把它揣进口袋，留着或有一日派作凭证。

那一带再看不出什么引人动问的东西，我便问自己：可有胆子下到地窖里去？末了断定，这在我这般处境的人，委实是超过所能指望的了，便朝四周潮湿荒凉的墙壁投去诀别的一瞥，然后如释重负，长出一口气，从厨房窗口一跃，重新跳进白昼的光与空气里。就在那时，我敢起誓，我听得老宅深处一扇门在铰链上转了一转，轻轻掩上了。我浑身一激灵，听出那声音出自地窖。

---

===Original===
My thoughts on the road back to Melville were many and conflicting. Chief above them all, however, rose the comfortable conclusion that in the pursuit of one mysterious affair, I had stumbled, as is often the case, upon the clue to another of yet greater importance, and by so doing got a start that might yet redound greatly to my advantage. For the reward offered for the recapture of the Schoenmakers was large, and the possibility of my being the one to put the authorities upon their track, certainly appeared after this day’s developements, open at least to a very reasonable hope. At all events I determined not to let the grass grow under my feet till I had informed the Superintendent of what I had seen and heard that day in the old haunt of these two escaped convicts.

Arrived at the public house in Melville, and learning that Mr. Blake had safely returned there an hour before, I drew the landlord to one side and asked what he could tell me about that old house of the two noted robbers Schoenmaker, I had passed on my way back among the hills.

“Wa’al now,” replied he, “this is curious. Here I’ve just been answering the gentleman upstairs a heap of questions concerning that self same old place, and now you come along with another batch of them; just as if that rickety old den was the only spot of interest we had in these parts.”

“Perhaps that may be the truth,” I laughed. “Just now when the papers are full of these rogues, anything concerning *them* must be of superior interest of course.” And I pressed him again to give me a history of the house and the two thieves who had inhabited it.

===Chinese===
回梅尔维尔的一路，我思绪纷纭，彼此顶撞。然而高出一切的，是一个惬意的结论：我在追究一桩疑案的当口，一如常情，撞上了另一桩分量更重的案子的线索，并由此抢得先手，日后或可大有利于我。因为缉回舍恩马克父子的赏格不菲，而由我引官府上他们踪迹的可能，经今日这番事态之后，至少也敞开着颇为合理的希望。无论如何，我打定主意，不把这 天在两个逃犯老巢中的所见所闻禀明局长，便决不让自己脚下长草。

到得梅尔维尔的客栈，听说布莱克先生一个钟头前已安然归来，我便把老板拉过一旁，问他就我归途山间经过的舍恩马克家那座老宅——两个有名大盗的老宅——能告诉我些什么。

“呣，这可就怪了，”他答道，“我这才回答完楼上那位先生一大堆关于这同一处老宅的问话，您又送来一批；倒好像那座摇摇晃晃的老贼窝，是咱们这一带唯一值得瞧的景致似的。”

“兴许真是这么回事呢，”我笑道。“眼下满报纸都是这两个家伙的消息，凡是关于*他们*的事，自然高人一等地有趣。”便又催他给我讲讲这宅子和住过它的两个窃贼的历史。

===Original===
“Wa’al,” drawled he, “ ’taint much we know about them, yet after all it may be a trifle too much for their necks some day. Time was when nobody thought especial ill of them beyond a suspicion or so of their being somewhat mean about money. That was when they kept an inn there, but when the robbery of the Rutland bank was so clearly traced to them, more than one man about here started up and said as how they had always suspected them Schoenmakers of being villains, and even hinted at something worse than robbery. But nothing beyond that one rascality has yet been proved against them, and for that they were sent to jail for twenty years as you know. Two months ago they escaped, and that is the last known of them. A precious set, too, they are; the father being only so much the greater rogue than the son as he is years older.”

“And the inn? When was that closed?”

“Just after their arrest.”

“Hasn’t it been opened since?”

===Chinese===
“呣，”他拖长了调子，“他们的事咱们知道得不多；不过说到底，兴许哪一天就是多得他们项上人头受不起的一丁点儿。早年间，除了疑心他们在银钱上头手紧些，谁也没把他们往坏处多想。那是他们还在那儿开客栈的时节。可待到拉特兰银行那桩劫案清清楚楚追到他们头上，这一带登时不止一个人跳起来，说自己早就疑心舍恩马克家不是好东西，甚而话里带话，点出比抢劫更黑的勾当。但除开那一桩恶行，到底没再坐实他们别的罪名；就为那一桩，您知道，他们判了二十年监禁。两个月前他们越了狱，此后便再无下落。也是一双宝贝：老子不过比儿子痴长几岁，为恶的本事，也就照着年岁，大上这么几分。”

“那么客栈呢？几时关的？”

“他们被拿之后不久就关了。”

“后来就没再开过？”

===Original===
“Only once when a brace of detectives came up from Troy to investigate, as they called it.”

“Who has the key?”

“Ah, that’s more than I can tell you.”

I dared not ask how my questions differed from those of Mr. Blake, nor indeed touch upon that point in any way. I was chiefly anxious now to return to New York without delay; so paying my bill I thanked the landlord, and without waiting for the stage, remounted my horse and proceeded at once to Putney where I was fortunate enough to catch the evening train. By five o’clock next morning I was in New York where I proceeded to carry out my programme by hastening at once to headquarters and reporting my suspicions regarding the whereabouts of the Schoenmakers. The information was received with interest and I had the satisfaction of seeing two men despatched north that very day with orders to procure the arrest of the two notable villains wherever found.

===Chinese===
“只开过一回，有两个特洛伊来的侦探北上，说是要查访查访。”

“钥匙在谁手里？”

“嗐，这我可就告诉不了您了。”

我不敢问我的问话同布莱克先生的有什么两样，也实在不敢朝那上头沾半点边。我如今一心只想赶紧回纽约；于是会了账，谢过老板，也不等驿车，重新上马，径奔帕特尼，幸而赶上了晚班火车。第二天早晨五点，我已在纽约，随即着手推行我的计划，火速赶往总局，把我关于舍恩马克父子下落的疑点呈报上去。这消息颇受重视，我满意地亲眼看见，当天便有两个人奉派北上，命令是：无论在何处撞见那两个赫赫有名的恶徒，即行拘拿。

## VIII A Word Overheard / 八 偶闻一言
===Original===
That evening I had a talk with Fanny over the area gate. She came out when she saw me approach, with her eyes staring and her whole form in a flutter.
“O,” she cried, “such things as I have heard this day!”
“Well,” said I, “what? let me hear too.” She put her hand on her heart. “I never was so frightened,” whispered she, “I thought I should have fainted right away. To hear that elegant lady use such a word as crime﻿—”
“What elegant lady?” interrupted I. “Don’t begin in the middle of your story, that’s a good girl; I want to hear it all.”

===Chinese===
那天傍晚，我隔着地下室前的矮栅门同范妮说话。她一见我走近便迎了出来，两眼圆睁，浑身激动。
“喔，”她嚷道，“我今天听见的事，可了不得！”
“呃，”我说，“什么事？也说给我听听。”她把手按在胸口。“我从没这么吓过，”她低声说，“当真险些当场晕了过去。听那位雍容的太太嘴里说出‘罪案’这样的字眼——”
“哪位雍容的太太？”我打断她。“别从半截讲起，乖姑娘；我要听个全乎。”

===Original===
“Well,” said she, calming down a little, “Mrs. Daniels had a visitor today, a lady. She was dressed﻿—”
“O, now,” interrupted I for the second time, “you can leave that out. Tell me what her name was and let the fol-de-rols go.”
“Her name?” exclaimed the girl with some sharpness, “how should I know her name; she didn’t come to see me.”
“How did she look then? You saw her I suppose?”
“And wasn’t that what I was telling you, when you stopped me. She looked like a queen, that she did; as grand a lady as ever I see, in her velvet dress sweeping over the floor, and her diamonds as big as﻿—”

===Chinese===
“是这么回事，”她说，稍稍平静了些，“丹尼尔斯太太今天来了位客，是位太太。她那身穿戴——”
“喔，得啦，”我第二次打断她，“穿戴就免了。只说那位太太姓甚名谁，那些花里胡哨的且搁下。”
“她姓甚名谁？”姑娘没好气地嚷道，“我哪里知道人家姓甚名谁；她又不是来看我的。”
“那么她生得什么模样？总看见了吧？”
“我方才要说的不正是这个么，偏叫你拦住了。她那模样像位王后，真真是位王后；我这辈子见过的顶气派的太太，一身丝绒裙子扫着地板走，那钻石，大得像——”

===Original===
“Was she a dark woman?” I asked.
“Her hair was black and so were her eyes, if that is what you mean.”
“And was she very tall and proud looking?”
The girl nodded. “You know her?” whispered she.
“No,” said I, “not exactly; but I think I can tell who she is. And so she called today on Mrs. Daniels, did she.”
“Yes, but I guess she knew master would be home before she got away.”

===Chinese===
“她是个深色皮肤的女人？”我问。
“她头发是黑的，眼睛也是黑的，倘若您问的是这个。”
“个子很高、样子很傲？”
姑娘点了点头。“您认得她？”她悄悄问。
“不，”我说，“算不上认得；不过我想得出她是谁。这么说，她今天来访的是丹尼尔斯太太，是么。”
“是，可我猜她早算准了，不等她走，老爷就该到家了。”

===Original===
“Come,” said I, “tell me all about it; I’m getting impatient.”
“And ain’t I telling you?” said she. “It was about three o’clock this afternoon, the time I go upstairs to dress, so I just hangs about in the hall a bit, near the parlor door, and I hear her gossiping with Mrs. Daniels almost as if she was an old friend, and Mrs. Daniels answering her mighty stiffly and as if she wasn’t glad to see her at all. But the lady didn’t seem to mind, but went on talking as sweet as honey, and when they came out, you would have thought she loved the old woman like a sister to see her look into her face and say something about knowing how busy she was, but that it would give her so much pleasure if she would come some day to see her and talk over old times. But Mrs. Daniels wasn’t pleased a bit and showed plain enough she didn’t like the lady, fine as she was in her ways. She was going to answer her too, but just then the front door opened and Mr. Blake with his satchel in his hand, came into the house. And how he did start, to be sure, when he saw them, though he tried to say something perlite which she didn’t seem to take to at all, for after muttering something about not expecting to see him, she put her hand on the knob and was going right out. But he stopped her and they went into the parlor together while Mrs. Daniels stood staring after them like one mad, her hand held out with his bag and umbrella in it, stiff as a statter in the Central Park. She didn’t stand so long, though, but came running down the hall, as if she was bewitched. I was dreadful flustered, for though I was hid behind the wall that juts out there by the back stairs, I was afraid she would see me and shame me before Mr. Blake. But she passed right by and never looked up. ‘There is something dreadful mysterious in this,’ thought I, and I just made up my mind to stay where I was till Mr. Blake and the lady should come out again from the parlor. I didn’t have to wait very long. In a few minutes the door opened and they stepped out, he ahead and she coming after. I thought this was queer, he is always so dreadful perlite in his ways, but I thought it was a deal queerer when I saw him go up the front stairs, she hurrying after, looking I cannot tell you how, but awful troubled and anxious, I should say.

===Chinese===
“来，”我说，“原原本本讲给我听；我可等得急了。”
“我不是正讲着么？”她说。“今天下午三点来钟，是我上楼梳妆的时辰，我便在厅堂里磨蹭了一会儿，就在客厅门边上，听见她正同丹尼尔斯太太闲谈，亲热得简直像多年老友，丹尼尔斯太太回话却硬邦邦的，一副全然不乐意见她的样子。可那位太太满不在乎，只管甜言蜜语地说下去；出来的时候，你简直要当她爱那老太太爱得像亲姊妹——瞧她凑着老太太的脸说，知道她如何忙碌，可她若哪天肯过去坐坐、叙叙旧，那真是给她天大的脸面。可丹尼尔斯太太一点不领情，把她的不喜欢写得明明白白，任凭那位太太做派多么讲究。她也正要回敬两句，恰在此时，前门开了，布莱克先生手里提着旅行袋走进屋来。他一见她们两个，那一份吃惊就别提了，尽管还想说两句客气话，她却全然不吃这一套，咕哝了句什么‘没想会碰见您’，手便搭上门钮，就要径直出去。可他拦住了她，两人一同进了客厅；丹尼尔斯太太提着他那袋子和伞，手直挺挺伸在半空，望着他们的背影发怔，活像中央公园里的一尊石像。不过她没站多久，便顺着厅堂一路跑来，像中了邪似的。我慌得要命，因为虽说躲在后面楼梯口凸出的那堵墙后头，我还是怕她瞧见我，当着布莱克先生的面下我的脸。可她径直跑过去了，眼皮也没抬。‘这事蹊跷得很，’我心里想，便打定主意守在原地，等布莱克先生和那位太太从客厅里再出来。没等多久。几分钟门就开了，两人走出来，他前头，她后头。我已觉得这事古怪——他平日那套客气功夫到哪里去了——及至看见他上前面楼梯、她急急跟上，那神色，我说不上来，总之是慌乱极了、着急极了，我就觉得越发古怪了。

===Original===
“They went into that room of his he calls his studio and though I knew it might cost me my place if I was found out, I couldn’t help following and listening at the keyhole.”
“And what did you hear?” I asked, for she paused to take breath.
“Well, the first thing I heard was a cry of pleasure from her, and the words, ‘You keep that always before you? You cannot dislike me, then, as much as you pretend.’ I don’t know what she meant nor what he did, but he stepped across the room and I heard her cry out this time as if she was hurt as well as awful surprised; and he talked and talked, and I couldn’t catch a word, he spoke so low; and by and by she sobbed just a little, and I got scared and would have run away but she cried out with a kind of shriek, ‘O, don’t say any more; to think that *crime* should come into our family, the proudest in the land. How could you, Holman, how could you.’ Yes,” the girl went on, flushing in her excitement till she was as red as the cherry ribbons in her cap, “those were the very words she used: ‘To think that crime should come into our family! the proudest one in the land!’ And she called him by his first name, and asked him how he could do it.”
“And what did Mr. Blake say?” returned I, a little taken back myself at this result of my efforts with Fanny.
“O, I didn’t wait to hear. I didn’t wait for anything. If folks was going to talk about such things as that, I thought I had better be anywhere than listening at the keyhole. I went right upstairs I can tell you.”

===Chinese===
“他们进了他称作画室的那间屋子；我明知道倘若被撞破，饭碗兴许就砸了，可还是忍不住跟过去，贴着锁眼听。”
“你听见了什么？”我问，因为她停下来喘气。
“是这样，我头一样听见的，是她一声欢喜的惊呼，还有那句话：‘您一直把它摆在眼前？那您就不见得像您装的那么讨厌我。’她指什么我不明白，他做了什么我也不明白，只听见他穿过屋子走过去，接着她又叫了一声，这一回像是又痛又吓；他一迭声地说呀说呀，声音低得我一个字也捉不住；过了半晌，她低低抽泣了一小会儿，我害了怕，正要溜走，她却近乎尖叫地喊了出来：‘喔，别再说了；想想看，*罪案*竟会进到咱们家门里来，全国数第一高贵的门第！您怎么能，霍尔曼，您怎么能。’是啊，”姑娘接着说，激动得满脸通红，红得同她帽子上樱桃色的缎带一般，“她用的正是这几个字：‘想想看，罪案竟会进到咱们家门里来！全国数第一高贵的门第！’她还直呼他的名字霍尔曼，问他怎么能干得出。”
“那么布莱克先生说什么了？”我反问，自己也不免为我对范妮下的这番功夫换来的结果吃了一惊。
“喔，我没等着听。什么也没等着听。人家既要谈那样的事，我想我随便待在哪里都好，就是不该贴在锁眼上听。我立马就上楼去了，真的。”

===Original===
“And whom have you told of what you heard in the half dozen hours that have gone by?”
“Nobody; how could you think so mean of me when I promised, and﻿—”
It is not necessary to go any further into this portion of the interview.
The Countess De Mirac possessed to its fullest extent the present fine lady’s taste for bric-a-brac. So much I had learned in my inquiries concerning her. Remembering this, I took the bold resolution of profiting by this weakness of hers to gain admission to her presence, she being the only one sharing Mr. Blake’s mysterious secret. Borrowing a valuable antique from a friend of mine at that time in the business, I made my appearance the very next day at her apartments, and sending in an urgent request to see Madame, by the trim negress who answered my summons, waited in some doubt for her reply.

===Chinese===
“这几个钟头里，你听见的这些话，又讲给谁听了？”
“谁也没告诉；我应承过的，您怎么把人想得这样不堪，还有——”
这段谈话的这一节，用不着再多细表。
时下风雅太太们嗜好古玩，德·米拉克伯爵夫人在这上头趣味十足。这是我在关于她的探询中得知的。记着这一层，我定下一个大胆的主意：乘她这桩癖好，求一个进见的机会——须知同布莱克先生共享那桩神秘秘密的，唯她一人。我向一位当时做这行买卖的朋友借了一件值钱的古董，第二天便登门到她的寓所，托那位应门的齐整黑人女仆递进去一封恳切的求见帖，心怀忐忑地等她回话。

===Original===
It came all too soon; Madame was ill and could see no one. I was not, however, to be baffled by one rebuff. Handing the basket I held to the girl, I urged her to take it in and show her mistress what it contained, saying it was a rare article which might never again come her way.
The girl complied, though with a doubtful shake of the head which was anything but encouraging. Her incredulity, however, must have been speedily rebuked, for she almost immediately returned without the basket, saying Madame would see me.
My first thoughts upon entering the grand lady’s presence, was that the girl had been mistaken, for I found the Countess walking the floor in an abstracted way, drying a letter she had evidently but just completed, by shaking it to and fro with an unsteady hand; the placque I had brought, lying neglected on the table.
But at sight of my respectful form standing with bent head in the doorway, she hurriedly thrust the letter into a book and took up the placque. As she did so I marked her well and almost started at the change I observed in her since that evening at the Academy. It was not only that she was dressed in some sort of loose deshabille that was in eminent contrast to the sweeping silks and satins in which I had hitherto beheld her adorned; or that she was laboring under some physical disability that robbed her dark cheek of the bloom that was its chiefest charm. The change I observed went deeper than that; it was more as if a light had been extinguished in her countenance. It was the same woman I had beheld standing like a glowing column of will and strength before the melancholy form of Mr. Blake, but with the will and strength gone, and with them all the glow.
“She no longer hopes,” thought I, and already felt repaid for my trouble.

===Chinese===
回话来得飞快：夫人贵体违和，概不见客。可我岂是一次回绝便打得退的。我把手里提的篮子递给那女仆，怂恿她拿进去给主人过目，说这是一件稀罕物，错过这村，再没这店。
女仆照办了，虽然摇着头，满脸的不信，一点也叫人提不起希望。不过她的不信想必立刻便受了训斥，因为她几乎马上就空着手回来了，说夫人愿意见我。
一进这位贵妇的房，我头一个念头便是：那女仆方才说谎。只见伯爵夫人正恍恍惚惚地在屋里踱步，手里抖抖索索地来回晃着一封信，晾干墨迹——那信分明刚写完；我带来的那只彩釉圆盘，孤零零撂在桌上，无人理会。
可一瞥见我低着头、恭恭敬敬立在门口的身影，她便慌忙把那封信塞进一本书里，拿起了那只彩盘。趁这当口，我把她细细端详了一番，险些惊跳起来：自音乐学院那一晚至今，她变得太多了。这还不单是她换了一身宽松的便装，同我先前见她珠围翠绕、曳着绸缎的光景判若两人；也不单是她身子显然违和，使那深色脸颊失去了最动人的红润。我看出的是更深一层的变故：仿佛她脸上有一盏灯熄了。还是那个女人，我曾见她像一根燃着意志与力量的通红柱子，立在愁容惨淡的布莱克先生面前；如今意志与力量都没了，随之一切光彩也都没了。
“她不再抱指望了，”我心里想，顿觉这一趟没有白跑。

===Original===
“This is a very pretty article you have brought me,” said she with something of the unrestrained love of art which she undoubtedly possessed, showing itself through all her languor. “Where did it come from, and what recommendations have you, to prove it is an honest sale you offer me?”
“None,” returned I, ignoring with a reassuring smile the first question, “except that I should not be afraid if all the police in New York knew I was here with this fine placque for sale.”
She gave a shrug of her proud shoulder that bespoke the French Countess and softly ran her finger round the edge of the placque.
“I don’t need anything more of this kind,” said she languidly; “besides,” and she set it down with a fretful air, “I am in no mood to buy this afternoon.” Then shortly, “What do you ask for it?”
I named a fabulous price.

===Chinese===
“你给我带来的这件东西，真够漂亮，”她说，那股子对艺术不加掩饰的热爱——这热爱她无疑是有——透过了她满身的慵懒。“它什么来路？你拿什么作保，证明你卖给我的是一桩正经买卖？”
“没有，”我答道，对头一个问题报以一个叫人放心的微笑，“只有一样：哪怕全纽约的警察都知道我带着这只漂亮的彩盘在这儿卖，我也不怕。”
她那高傲的肩膀一耸，耸出一副法国伯爵夫人的派头，手指却轻轻顺着彩盘的边沿抚了一圈。
“这类东西我一件也不缺了，”她懒懒地说；“再说，”她把彩盘烦躁地一撂，“今天下午我没有买兴。”随即短短地问：“你要价多少？”
我报了个天价。

===Original===
She started and cast me a keen glance. “You had better take it to someone else; I have no money to throw away.”
With a hesitating hand I lifted the placque towards the basket. “I would very much like to sell it to you,” said I. “Perhaps﻿—”
Just then a lady’s fluttering voice rose from the room beyond inquiring for the Countess, and hurriedly taking the placque from my hand with an impulsive “O there’s Amy,” she passed into the adjoining apartment, leaving the door open behind her.
I saw a quick interchange of greetings between her and a fashionably dressed lady, then they withdrew to one side with the ornament I had brought, evidently consulting in regard to its merits. Now was my time. The book in which she had placed the letter she had been writing lay on the table right before me, not two inches from my hand. I had only to throw back the cover and my curiosity would be satisfied. Taking advantage of a moment when their backs were both turned, I pressed open the book with a careful hand, and with one eye on them and one on the sheet before me, managed to read these words:﻿—

===Chinese===
她一惊，锐利地瞟了我一眼。“你还是拿到别处去吧；我没有闲钱可糟蹋。”
我迟疑着手，把彩盘往篮子里挪。“我很愿意把它卖给夫人，”我说。“也许——”
正在这时，里间传来一位太太叽叽喳喳的声音，问伯爵夫人在不在；她一时冲动，口里说着“喔，艾米来了”，慌忙从我手里拿过彩盘，走进隔壁屋子，门也没带上。
我看见她同一位衣着入时的太太飞快地寒暄了一阵，随后两人带着我送来的那件古物退到一边，显然在品评它的好处。我的时机到了。她方才放信的那本书，就摊在我面前的桌上，离我的手不到两寸。只消掀开封面，我的好奇心便可满足。趁两人都背过身去的一瞬，我小心翼翼地掀开书页，一眼盯着她们，一眼盯着面前那页信笺，读到了这么几句：——

===Original===
> My Dearest Cecilia.
> I have tried in vain to match the sample you sent me at Stewart’s, Arnold’s and McCreery’s. If you still insist upon making up the dress in the way you propose, I will see what Madame Dudevant can do for us, though I cannot but advise you to alter your plans and make the darker shade of velvet do. I went to the Cary reception last night and met Lulu Chittenden. She has actually grown old, but was as lively as ever. She created a great stir in Paris when she was there; but a husband who comes home two o’clock in the morning with bleared eyes and empty pockets, is not conducive to the preservation of a woman’s beauty. How she manages to retain her spirits I cannot imagine. You ask me news of cousin Holman. I meet him occasionally and he looks well, but has grown into the most sombre man you ever saw. In regard to certain hopes of which you have sometimes made mention, let me assure you they are no longer practicable. He has done what﻿—

===Chinese===
> 我最亲爱的塞西莉亚：
> 你寄来的料子样，我在斯图尔特、阿诺德、麦克里几家商行都配过，白费了力气。你若还是执意照你说的式样做这件衣裳，我便去看看杜德万太太能替我们想什么法子，只是我不得不劝你改改主意，就用那颜色深些的天鹅绒将就了吧。昨晚我去了卡里家的招待会，遇见了露露·奇滕登。她当真见老了，人却还同从前一样活泼。她在巴黎那阵子很是轰动过一场；只是做丈夫的凌晨两点钟才回家，眼睛熬得通红，口袋空空如也，这于保住女人的容颜最是没有好处。她怎么还能那样有精神，我真想不透。你问我表哥霍尔曼的近况。我偶尔碰见他，气色不坏，人却变得郁郁寡欢，你从来没见过那么阴沉的男子。至于你有时提起过的那几桩指望，我且拿实话说给你：都不再有望了。他已经做出了那样的事——

===Original===
Here the conversation ceased in the other room, the Countess made a movement of advance and I closed the book with an inward groan over my ill-luck.
“It is very pretty,” said she with a weary air; “but as I remarked before, I am not in the buying mood. If you will take half you mention, I may consider the subject, but﻿—”
“Pardon me, Madame,” I interrupted, being in no wise anxious to leave the placque behind me, “I have been considering the matter and I hold to my original price. Mr. Blake of Second Avenue may give it to me if you do not.”
“Mr. Blake!” She eyed me suspiciously. “Do you sell to him?”

===Chinese===
读到此处，里间的谈话停了，伯爵夫人挪步要走，我心里哀叹着自家时运不济，把书合上了。
“这东西确实漂亮，”她一脸倦容地说；“可是我说过了，我没有买兴。你若肯减半价，这桩买卖我兴许还能考虑，不过——”
“恕我直言，夫人，”我打断道，全无把彩盘留下的意思，“我又想了想，价钱还是原价。您若不要，第二大道的布莱克先生兴许肯出。”
“布莱克先生！”她狐疑地打量我。“你也卖东西给他？”

===Original===
“I sell to anyone I can,” replied I; “and as he has an artist’s eye for such things﻿—”
Her brows knitted and she turned away. “I do not want it;” said she, “sell it to whom you please.”
I took up the placque and left the room.

===Chinese===
“谁肯买我便卖给谁，”我答道；“何况他对这类东西，独具一双艺术家的眼光——”
她眉头一皱，转过脸去。“我不要了；”她说，“你爱卖给谁卖给谁。”
我拿起彩盘，出了那间屋子。

## IX A Few Golden Hairs / 九 几根金发
===Original===
When a few days from that I made my appearance before Mr. Gryce, it was to find him looking somewhat sober. “Those Schoenmakers,” said he, “are making a deal of trouble. It seems they escaped the fellows up north and are now somewhere in this city, but where﻿—”
An expressive gesture finished the sentence.
“Is that so?” exclaimed I. “Then we are sure to nab them. Given time and a pair of low, restless German thieves, I will wager anything, our hands will be upon them before the month is over. I only hope, when we do come across them, it will not be to find their betters too much mixed up with their devilish practices.” And I related to him what Fanny had told me a few evenings before.
“The coil is tightening,” said he. “What the end will be I don’t know. *Crime*, said she? I wish I knew in what blind hole of the earth that girl we are after lies hidden.”
As if in answer to this wish the door opened and one of our men came in with a letter in his hand. “Ha!” exclaimed Mr. Gryce, after he had perused it, “look at that.”
I took the letter from his hand and read:

===Chinese===
过了几天，我到格赖斯先生跟前去见他，发觉他神色颇为凝重。“舍恩马克家那两个，”他说，“正给我们惹着不小的麻烦。看样子他们从北边那班人手里溜掉了，如今就藏在这城里什么地方，可是到底在哪儿——”
一个意味深长的手势替他说完了这句话。
“当真？”我喊道。“那我们准能拿下他们。只要工夫给足，凭着一对下作、躁动的日耳曼窃贼——我随便拿什么打赌，不出月底，他们便落在我们手里。我只盼到时候撞见他们，别撞见比他们体面的人也深深搅在他们那套鬼蜮勾当里。”于是我把几天前一个晚上范妮告诉我的话，对他讲了一遍。
“罗网正在收紧，”他说。“结局如何，我不知道。她说了*罪案*两个字，是么？但愿我能知道，我们追索的这个姑娘，到底藏在地上哪个黑洞里。”
仿佛应着这个心愿，门开了，我们的一名手下手里拿着一封信进来。“哈！”格赖斯先生读罢喊道，“你瞧这个。”
我从他手里接过信来，念道：

===Original===
> The dead body of a girl such as you describe was found in the East river off Fiftieth Street this morning. From appearance has been dead some time. Have telegraphed to Police Headquarters for orders. Should you wish to see the body before it is removed to the Morgue or otherwise disturbed, please hasten to Pier 48 E.R.
> Graham.

===Chinese===
> 今晨在东河五十街外水面发现一具女尸，其状如您所描述。从外表看已死去多时。已电请警察总局指示。您如欲在尸体移存陈尸所或作其他处置之前验看，请速赴东河 48 号码头。
> 格雷厄姆

===Original===
“Come,” said I, “let’s go and see for ourselves. If it should be the one﻿—”
“The dinner party proposed by Mr. Blake for tonight, may have its interruptions,” he remarked.
I do not wish to make my story any longer than is necessary, but I must say that when in an hour or so later, I stood with Mr. Gryce before the unconscious form of that poor drowned girl I felt an unusual degree of awe stealing over me: there was so much mystery connected with this affair, and the parties implicated were of such standing and repute.
I almost dreaded to see the covering removed from her face lest I should behold, what? I could not have told if I had tried.
“A trim made body enough,” cried the official in charge as Mr. Gryce lifted an end of the cloth that enveloped her and threw it back. “Pity the features are not better preserved.”
“No need for us to see the features,” exclaimed I, pointing to the locks of golden red hair that hung in tangled masses about her. “The hair is enough; she is not the one.” And I turned aside, asking myself if it was relief I felt.

===Chinese===
“走，”我说，“咱们亲自去看看。倘若真是那一个——”
“布莱克先生今晚张罗的晚宴，怕要被打断了，”他说。
我不愿把故事拉得比必要的更长，但必须说一句：约莫一个钟头之后，当我同格赖斯先生站在那可怜的溺亡姑娘那不省人事的身形前时，一阵异样的敬畏之感悄悄爬上我的心头：这案子的谜团如此之多，牵连的诸人又都是那等身份名望。
我几乎怕人揭开她脸上的遮盖，生怕看见——看见什么呢？我便是想，也说不出来。
“身段倒齐整，”负责的官员喊道，这时格赖斯先生掀起裹着她的布单的一角，撩了回去。“可惜五官保存得差了些。”
“不必看五官了，”我喊道，一面指着垂在她头边乱蓬蓬的一大绺一大绺金红色头发。“这头发就尽够了；她不是那一个。”我转过身去，心里自问，方才那阵子究竟算不算如释重负。

===Original===
To my surprise Mr. Gryce did not follow.
“Tall, thin, white face, black eyes.” I heard him whisper to himself. “It *is* a pity the features are not better preserved.”
“But,” said I, taking him by the arm, “Fanny spoke particularly of her hair being black, while this girl’s﻿—Good heavens!” I suddenly ejaculated as I looked again at the prostrate form before me. “Yellow hair or black, this is the girl I saw him speaking to that day in Broome Street. I remember her clothes if nothing more.” And opening my pocketbook, I took out the morsel of cloth I had plucked that day from the ash barrel, lifted up the discolored rags that hung about the body and compared the two. The pattern, texture and color were the same.
“Well,” said Mr. Gryce, pointing to certain contusions, like marks from the blow of some heavy instrument on the head and bared arms of the girl before us; “he will have to answer me one question anyhow, and that is, who this poor creature is who lies here the victim of treachery or despair.” And turning to the official he asked if there were any other signs of violence on the body.
The answer came deliberately, “Yes, she has evidently been battered to death.”
Mr. Gryce’s lips closed with grim decision. “A most brutal murder,” said he and lifting up the cloth with a hand that visibly trembled, he softly covered her face.

===Chinese===
出乎意料，格赖斯先生却没有跟上来。
“高个子，瘦，白脸，黑眼睛。”我听见他自言自语。“五官没保存好，*实在*可惜。”
“可是，”我说，一面挽住他的胳膊，“范妮分明说她的头发是黑的，而这姑娘的——老天爷！”我又朝面前那具僵卧的身子看了一眼，突然失声喊了出来。“管她黄头发黑头发，这正是那天我在布卢姆街看见他搭话的那个姑娘。别的不敢说，她那身衣裳我记得。”说着打开皮夹，取出那天从垃圾箱上扯下的一片布角，掀起尸体上挂着的变了色的破衣，两下一比：花样、布纹、颜色，一模一样。
“唉，”格赖斯先生说，一面指着姑娘头上和裸露的胳膊上的几处淤伤，那像是被什么沉重家伙打的印子；“无论如何，他总得回答我一个问题了：躺在这里的这可怜人究竟是谁，她是遭了奸计，还是绝了生路。”说罢他转向那官员，问尸体上可还有别的暴力的痕迹。
回答来得沉着：“有，她分明是被人活活打死的。”
格赖斯先生的嘴唇铁一般地抿紧了。“一桩凶残已极的谋杀，”他说着，抬起那块布单——那只手抖得分明可见——轻轻覆上她的脸。

===Original===
“Well,” said I as we slowly paced back up the pier, “there is one thing certain, she is not the one who disappeared from Mr. Blake’s house.”
“I am not so sure of that.”
“How!” said I. “You believed Fanny lied when she gave that description of the missing girl upon which we have gone till now?”
Mr. Gryce smiled, and turning back, beckoned to the official behind us. “Let me have that description,” said he, “which I distributed among the Harbor Police some days ago for the identification of a certain corpse I was on the lookout for.”
The man opened his coat and drew out a printed paper which at Mr. Gryce’s word he put into my hand. It ran as follows:

===Chinese===
“好了，”我们沿着码头慢慢往回走时，我说，“有一件事是拿得准的：她不是从布莱克先生家失踪的那一个。”
“这我可不敢说。”
“怎么！”我说。“你认为范妮描述那失踪姑娘的模样时说了谎？我们如今一直是照着她的描述办事的！”
格赖斯先生笑了笑，回身招了招跟在我们身后的那位官员。“把那份通缉描述给我，”他说，“前几天我分发到水上警察那儿去的，为的是认明一具我正留意的尸首。”
那人解开外衣，抽出一张印好的纸，依着格赖斯先生的话递到我手里。纸上写道：

===Original===
> Look out for the body of a young girl, tall, well shaped but thin, of fair complexion and golden hair of a peculiar bright and beautiful color, and when found, acquaint me at once.
> G.

“I don’t understand,” began I.
But Mr. Gryce tapping me on the arm said in his most deliberate tones, “Next time you examine a room in which anything of a mysterious nature has occurred, look under the bureau and if you find a comb there with several long golden hairs tangled in it, be very sure before you draw any definite conclusions, that your Fannys know what they are talking about when they declare the girl who used that comb had black hair on her head.”

===Chinese===
> 注意查找一年轻女子尸体：高个，身段端正而瘦削，肤色白皙，金发，其色鲜亮殊异；寻获后，即刻知会本人。
> 格

“我不明白，”我开口道。
可格赖斯先生拍了拍我的胳膊，用他最从容不迫的腔调说：“下回你再检查一间出过什么神秘事情的屋子，记得看看五斗橱底下；倘若你在那里发现一把梳子，缠着几根长长的金发，那么，在你得出任何断语之前，先弄弄清楚：你的那些范妮们一口咬定用这把梳子的姑娘满头黑发时，她们说的到底是不是实话。”

## X The Secret of Mr. Blake’s Studio / 十 布莱克先生画室的秘密

===Original===
“Mr. Blake is at dinner, sir, with company, but I will call him if you say so.”

“No,” returned Mr. Gryce; “show us into some room where we can be comfortable and we will wait till he has finished.”

The servant bowed, and stepping forward down the hall, opened the door of a small and cosy room heavily hung with crimson curtains. “I will let him know that you are here,” said he, and vanished towards the dining-room.

“I doubt if Mr. Blake will enjoy the latter half of his bill of fare as much as the first,” said I, drawing up one of the luxurious armchairs to the side of my principal. “I wonder if he will break away from his guests and come in here?”

“No; if I am not mistaken we shall find Mr. Blake a man of nerve. Not a muscle of his face will show that he is disturbed.”

===Chinese===
“布莱克先生正在用饭，先生，还有客人在座；不过您吩咐一声，我就去请他。”

“不必，”格赖斯先生答道；“领我们到一间坐得舒服的屋子去，我们等他吃完。”

仆人躬了躬身，赶前几步走过穿堂，推开一间小而舒适的屋子的门，屋里重重悬着绛红的帷幔。“我这就去回禀他二位到了，”说着，便朝饭厅那边去了。

“我疑心布莱克先生后半席的菜，吃不出前半席那个滋味来了，”我说着，把一张华贵的扶手椅拖到我上司身边。“也不知他会不会撇下客人，到这屋里来？”

“不会；若是我没有看错，我们将会发现布莱克先生是个有胆量的人。他脸上连一块肌肉也不会露出心里的不安。”

===Original===
“Well,” said I, “I dread it.”

Mr. Gryce looked about on the gorgeous walls and the rich old fashioned furniture that surrounded him, and smiled one of his grimmest smiles.

“Well, you may,” said he.

The next instant a servant stood in the doorway, bearing to our great astonishment, a tray well set with decanter and glasses.

“Mr. Blake’s compliments, gentlemen,” said he, setting it down on the table before us. “He hopes you will make yourselves at home and he will see you as soon as possible.”

===Chinese===
“唉，”我说，“我心里发怵。”

格赖斯先生环顾四周华丽的墙面和考究的老式家具，绽出他最狰狞的一种微笑。

“怵就怵吧，”他说。

转眼间一名仆人立在门口，托着一盘考究摆好的细颈酒瓶和酒杯，令我们大吃一惊。

“布莱克先生谨致问候，二位先生，”他说着，把托盘放在我们面前的桌上。“他盼二位不必拘束，他尽快就来。”

===Original===
The *humph!* of Mr. Gryce when the servant had gone would have done your soul good, also the look he cast at the pretty Dresden Shepherdess on the mantelpiece, as I reached out my hand towards the decanter. Somehow it made me draw back.

“I think we had better leave his wine alone,” said he.

And for half an hour we sat there, the wine untouched between us, listening alternately to the sound of speechmaking and laughter that came from the dining-room, and the solemn ticking of the clock as it counted out the seconds on the mantelpiece. Then the guests came in from the table, filing before us past the open door on their way to the parlors. They were all gentlemen of course﻿—Mr. Blake never invited ladies to his house﻿—and gentlemen of well known repute. The dinner had been given in honor of a certain celebrated statesman, and the character of his guests was in keeping with that of the one thus complimented.

As they went by us gaily indulging in the jokes and light banter with which such men season a social dinner, I saw Mr. Gryce’s face grow sober by many a shade; and when in the midst of it all, we heard the voice of Mr. Blake rise in that courteous and measured tone for which it is distinguished, I saw him reach forward and grasp his cane with an uneasiness I had never seen displayed by him before. But when some time later, the guests having departed, the dignified host advanced with some apology to where we were, I never beheld a firmer look on Mr. Gryce’s face than that with which he rose and confronted him. Mr. Blake’s own had not more character in it.

“You have called at a rather inauspicious time, Mr. Gryce,” said the latter, glancing at the card which he held in his hand. “What may your business be? Something to do with politics, I suppose.”

===Chinese===
